Thu Aug 20, 2026: ExlService Holdings Expands Credit Capacity: A New Era for AI Investments?

ExlService Holdings, Inc. has taken a significant step in its financial strategy by entering into a new US$1.00 billion senior secured credit agreement with a syndicate led by PNC Bank, as announced on August 18, 2026. This move replaces its previous Citibank facility and extends revolving and term loan maturities to August 18, 2031.

The new credit facility is designed to provide ExlService greater balance sheet flexibility, allowing the company to fund acquisitions, technology investments, and share repurchases. It features leverage-linked pricing and covenants that impose defined limits on debt and interest coverage, which enhances the company's ability to pursue its ambitious goals in the AI sector.

As ExlService Holdings pivots from traditional outsourcing to a focus on data and AI, the new US$1.00 billion credit line appears to support the ongoing narrative of AI-driven growth. This expanded credit capacity is expected to bolster ExlService's strategy to secure AI-led deal wins while managing the associated risks, particularly those arising from rising wage inflation and compliance costs, which could complicate pricing power.

The recent acquisition of iMerit, which has brought its CEO into ExlService's executive team, further underscores the importance of scaling AI data services. Together with the larger credit facility, this acquisition highlights the company's commitment to enhancing its capabilities in this high-demand sector, while also raising concerns related to execution risks in integrating new talent and maintaining resilient operating margins.

Analysts are optimistic about ExlService Holdings' growth potential, projecting revenues of approximately US$3.2 billion and earnings of around US$374.5 million by 2029. The new credit facility could reinforce this growth outlook; however, if cost pressures intensify more than anticipated, it may lead to a reassessment of the feasibility of meeting these targets.

SECTION 2 CORPORATE PROFILE

2.1 Activities

ExlService Holdings, Inc., a Nasdaq-listed company, is a global leader in data analytics, digital operations, and consulting services, primarily serving industries such as insurance, healthcare, banking, and retail. The company's core business revolves around leveraging advanced analytics, artificial intelligence, and machine learning to optimize operations, enhance decision-making, and drive efficiency for its clients. By transforming complex data into actionable insights, ExlService helps organizations streamline processes and mitigate risks. Among its fastest-growing segments are analytics-driven solutions and digital transformation services, fueled by increasing demand for AI-powered tools and cloud-based platforms. This focus has enabled the company to expand its client base and achieve strong revenue growth, positioning it as a key player in the evolving landscape of business process outsourcing and technology-enabled services. ExlService maintains a commitment to innovation and client-centric strategies, fostering sustainable growth in a competitive market. It is the NASDAQ's 6th largest Business services company by market capitalization.

### 26. [Axios C-Suite: The 6 dimensions of AI leadership](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60746859626&p=21l&v=1&x=RS_nhGcaNwAcfxfscuG0Jg)
- **Outlet(s):** Axios | **Published:** `2026-10-31` | **Sentiment:** `NEUTRAL` | **Positioning Relevance Score:** `0.60`
- **Syndicated URLs:** [link](https://www.axios.com/2026/10/31/ceo-ai-leadership-tips)
- **Matched Competitors:** Palantir, Turing
- **Coverage Snippet:** October 31st, 2026 ( Axios - Delivered by Newstex )

We're all AI CEOs now.

Why it matters: AI isn't a choice for us. It's an urgent enabler of revenue, growth, cost and confusion that many aren't remotely ready for.

I've heard from two companies knee-deep in AI tutoring of CEOs that told me at best 15% of CEOs are AI-savvy. Worse, most board chairs know even less about the technology.

This isn't easy. All of us are leading AI-enabled companies that demand SIX new layers of AI leadership and expertise.

We need to understand it, use and explain it, govern it, implement and measure it, restructure our teams and companies to exploit it, and then make sure our companies and products show up inside of it.

‼️ Very important: I'm going to outline the six layers today and then use my Axios C-Suite newsletter going forward ( ask to join here) to help us master each layer by synthesizing what's working inside AI-savvy companies.

Understand it. We've got to master what AI is, how it works, what it unlocks, what's unfolding with technological advances, what's likely to come, and what our competitors are doing better than us. The CEO cannot outsource this. It will soon be a vital part of EVERY decision you make. Here's a list of trustworthy tutors to follow.

Use and explain it. You can't understand its capabilities without using it and prototyping with it. You only see the magic when you work persistently on various problems and ideas and fine-tune it to understand your brain and company. I hired a personal AI savant to work with me outside our formal structure. This will help you guide and teach your staff. This must come from you, at least broadly. Your staff is desperate for leadership and reassurance that you will unleash it smartly and safely.

Govern it. YOU decide who owns AI, what's off-limits, and how the board and staff sees it even as it rapidly evolves. The specifics will fall to your CTO or CIO. But your board and company need to understand broadly the evolving thinking on AI and agent use at every level of the company. Most boards are flying blind on this one. 71% of directors named AI as the top skill their boards need to strengthen, and 82% said information on AI's link to risk and business performance was fair, poor or unavailable.

Implement and track it. This is hard as hell right now because the technology evolves so quickly and hits internal snags so persistently. Think of this as a living, always-evolving, never-ending project. Oh, and it changes WEEKLY. Your team needs to know with precision what can and should be automated now or soon, and then how humans and agents will collaborate to do it and monitor it. This is tedious, painful work.

Restructure for it. You think implementation is hard? Just wait for the restructuring it demands. We will all be human/agent-hybrid companies operating at a super-velocity never before seen. I'm three years into this and tinkering with structure CONSTANTLY. It's my new obsession. You will find you need to flatten your org fast to decrease idea-to-implementation speed. And rethink CIO, CTO, CPO to find one person with responsibility for understanding your proprietary data and information, your tech and security stack - as well as AI enablement companywide.

Show up inside of it. Most CEOs are clueless on this one. All of us need to restructure our content, marketing and messaging to surface smartly inside LLMs and agents. The CEO of Profound, the leading company monitoring this new dynamic, recently provided C-Suite readers tips on fixing this fast.

The big picture: This is A LOT, especially on top of our non-AI responsibilities. There are a lot of consultancies - Accenture, Teneo, Thoughtworks, FSG - helping CEOs with some pieces of this.

Truth is, we all need a holistic solution.

If you're a CEO or on a CEO's team: Ask to join Jim's Axios C-Suite newsletter so you can go deeper in the weeks ahead on these six dimensions of AI leadership.

### 30. [FedEx CISO: Vet AI agents with guardrails first](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60741928335&p=21l&v=1&x=ZjaZWHv76pmS3u6lMRUetA)
- **Outlet(s):** InformationWeek | **Published:** `2026-10-30` | **Sentiment:** `NEUTRAL` | **Positioning Relevance Score:** `0.60`
- **Matched Competitors:** Palantir, Appen
- **Coverage Snippet:** NEW YORK - The tide of AI agents flooding the enterprise continues to rise, but that inevitable wave must still be vetted to ensure they operate within enterprise guardrails, according to Gene Sun, corporate vice president and CISO at FedEx. For the logistics and transportation company, which brings in almost $86 billion in annual revenue, that means AI agents introduced to enterprise ecosystems must come from within and be cleared by security, according to Sun. "Even employee-created, self-created agents are internally based on our own platform. We have to register it.

We have to know about it," Sun told InformationWeek after delivering the keynote at the recent ONUG AI Networking Summit. Strict control over where and how AI agents are deployed is necessary, especially given reports of agents exploiting loopholes in guardrails in order to fulfill commands. That means observability is mandatory and the originator of an agent's actions must be known, Sun said. Related:FINRA CIO strives for effectiveness over efficiency in AI usage "Every single action of agents must have agency traced to human beings," he said. How FedEx meshes AI with the real world During the keynote, Sun was joined onstage by Arvind Rajagopalan, vice president of data and AI at FedEx, where they discussed AI at a global scale while maintaining security and control. FedEx's use of AI converges with the real world as it ships 18 million parcels daily in about 220 countries and territories around the globe. Sun explained how digital and physical realms function in tandem. "AI cannot replace our airplanes, our trucks, our distribution network," he said. However, the data associated with packages FedEx ships can provide insight into global economics. "We're going to leverage AI to create additional enterprise value," Sun said. In addition to processing that data, Rajagopalan said the digital side of the business includes assessing FedEx's vehicles for maintenance to keep deliveries flowing. "Our strategy has been to work on building out a real-time digital twin that gives us visibility into the entire network, what is happening at any given point in time with every single element," he said. FedEx deployed AI to assist with monitoring and prescribing preventive maintenance for the many moving parts across the shipper's network. Further, keeping appraised of external factors such as weather around the world also factors into FedEx's planning, Rajagopalan said. That data is fed to AI to assist with decision-making to ensure deliveries arrive on schedule. "This is helping us tremendously in terms of keeping our network fresh, scaled, as well as robust," he said. Related:InformationWeek Podcast: Is AI the best watchdog for the supply chain? Arvind Rajagopalan, vice president of data and AI at FedEx. [Joao-Pierre S. Ruth/InformationWeek] AI has also become a critical component for business strategy. Sun did not play coy about how top-of-mind the technology is for upper management, as well as the need to show the technology can be trusted. "Absolutely every single board meeting, AI is front and center ... no doubt about it," he said. FedEx's board wants to know what kind of AI use cases are being deployed and how the technology interacts with corporate systems, Sun said. That includes asking about governance, as AI may present recommendations to customers or set actions in motion across the company's operations. "Do we know what the AI model is doing? If a regulator or customer asks questions, can we explain that AI action?" Sun asked. FedEx's leadership also wants to know the value AI delivers beyond proof of concept, he said. They're interested in understanding how an AI project could scale - and how quickly. That requires clean and accurate data so AI models can function with complete context to deliver results, Sun said. Securing AI at the start Rajagopalan added that conversations about the benefits of AI also include discussions of risks and how to establish guardrails early on. "While there is a massive push and focus on getting things done and scaling AI across enterprises, it is very, very important to keep things secure," he said. Related:How can CIOs course-correct AI for inherited human biases? FedEx is actively looking at two different types of AI to scale across two different areas, Rajagopalan said. The first focuses on productivity tools for the company's more than 450,000 employees. In December, FedEx announced its multi-year partnership with Accenture to provide AI literacy training across the company. The second is a plan to launch AI agents to work in business processes. Rajagopalan cited FedEx CEO Raj Subramaniam's goal to see 50% of the company's workflows "agentified" as soon as possible. "That is front and center in terms of how we think ... and what we are looking to get done in the next couple of years," Rajagopalan said.
